It has to be replayed at cutover like any other migration, and it appends rather than replaces so the fee app keeps `public` |
| D-20 | `getCurrentStaff()` is cached per request, not across requests | The fee app caches the profile for 60s because of its traffic; this app has a few dozen users, so a role change or a deactivation should take effect on the next navigation |
| D-21 | Signed in with no staff profile is a named state, not an error | VPPS One shares `auth.users` with the fee app, so any fee-app account can sign in here and succeed. The login page says why instead of failing blankly |
| D-22 | Migration files are named for the version the Supabase MCP recorded | The MCP assigns its own timestamp. Naming the file to match keeps the repo and the database's history in exact correspondence for the cutover replay |
| D-23 | The proxy sends signed-out visitors to `/login`, but never sends signed-in ones away from it | The proxy has a JWT, not a profile. Bouncing on the JWT loops forever against a session whose profile was deactivated, and every hop runs the page. The login page already decides this from the profile, so one authority instead of two disagreeing ones |
| D-24 | The design system is the fee app's "Ledger Calm" palette, ported to Tailwind v4 `@theme` | The same staff use both apps on the same phones. A second visual language is a second thing to learn, for no gain |
| D-25 | Signed-in screens live in the `src/app/(app)/` route group, guarded by its layout | Makes the guard structural: a new screen is protected by where its file sits, not by somebody remembering a call at the top of it |
| D-26 | Navigation is derived from `can()`, and each item carries a `ready` flag | One map for access and for navigation, so they cannot drift. `ready` exists because the phase ships over several deploys — a tab whose route has not landed is a 404 with the school's name on it. A test asserts the flag matches a real page file, in both directions |
| D-27 | Pages use `requirePermission()` (redirects); server actions use `assertPermission()` (throws) | `redirect()` works by throwing a navigation signal, which a `try/catch` inside an action would swallow — and a swallowed guard is a guard that passed |
| D-28 | A navigation bar with fewer than two tabs is not rendered | Its only job is showing where else you could go. With one tab it says nothing and costs 68px of a phone screen |
| D-29 | Staff search filters in the browser, over the whole list | A school has a few dozen staff. One fetch and instant filtering beats a round trip per keystroke, and it keeps working where the signal is worst — which is the corridor, which is where it is used |
| D-30 | A phone number that is not a 10-digit Indian mobile is refused, not stored as typed | Phase 5 messages these numbers. A wrong number that looks right is worse than a blank |
| D-31 | Staff are deactivated, never deleted | The timetable, attendance and marks all reference the profile, and `short_name` is the join key. Inactive means "not in the pickers", not "never existed" |
| D-32 | Fee-table reads go through the service-role client, after `assertPermission()` in the repository function itself | The fee app's RLS gates `public.classes` and `public.students` on `has_any_permission()`, which reads `public.users.role`. VPPS One staff have no row there (D-6), so a cookie-client read returns zero rows **and no error** — which renders as "the school has no classes". A policy would fix it and rule 2 forbids adding one to a fee table, so the guard moves into the app. It sits in the repository rather than the caller so a future call site cannot forget it |
| D-33 | The class-label editor saves one row at a time | Nineteen classes get filled in over more than one sitting. A single form with one Save at the bottom loses the lot to an interruption |
| D-34 | The academic session is derived from the fee app's class rows, not configured | There is no `public.sessions` table; the label lives on each class. Deriving it means the cutover follows the real session with no variable to remember. `VPPSONE_SESSION_LABEL` overrides it for the weeks when two sessions overlap |
| D-35 | Language is a property of the account — `hi`, `en` or `both` — not of the app | Janmejay's call. The school is not one audience: a teacher in the village reads Hindi, the office reads English. Showing everybody both made every label twice as long for every person and gave nobody the screen they wanted. Strings are written once as a pair and rendered per reader |
| D-36 | The login screen's language comes from a device cookie written at sign-in; a first-ever visit gets `both` | Signed out there is no profile to read. Bilingual is the only choice that cannot lock somebody out of the one screen they must be able to read. Ported from the fee app, which reconciles `preferred_locale` to a cookie for the same reason |
| D-37 | Dense surfaces — nav tabs, list rows, the browser tab title — lead with one language even for a `both` reader | A tab is ~70px and a title bar truncates. Two lines there is not bilingual, it is unreadable. Detail views, notices and form labels show both |
| D-38 | Validation messages cross the Zod boundary as JSON-encoded pairs | A schema is parsed in a server action, long before a component exists, so it cannot know the reader's language. JSON rather than a delimiter because a name or class label could contain any separator, and a message that splits in the wrong place is worse than one left untranslated |
| D-39 | A calendar day says *both* whether school is open and whether the timetable runs | They are not the same question. On an exam or an event day children come in and the register is taken, but no period runs. Collapsing the two would mean either an exam day with no attendance recorded, or a holiday that still expects a register |
| D-40 | Only non-ordinary days are stored; absence of a row means the weekly pattern | A year is a few dozen rows rather than 365, and adding a holiday is one insert. The weekly pattern is Sunday closed, **Saturday open** — the thing a general-purpose calendar library gets wrong for this school |
| D-41 | A class-teacher handover closes the old row on the day before the new one starts | Two open rows would mean "who is the class teacher today" has two answers. The partial unique index refuses it, and the close-then-open order means a failure leaves a class visibly empty rather than permanently blocked |
| D-42 | A calendar range is expanded to one row per day, not stored as a range | Every other screen asks about one day — "is school open on the 14th". A stored range would make that question a join, on the hottest read in Phase 3 |
| D-43 | A rebuild never deletes an enrolment whose student the fee app has lost | That child still has attendance and marks recorded here. Dropping the row would orphan history that is still true, so the consistency check reports it and a person decides |
| D-44 | Enrolments are rebuilt on a button, never on a schedule | A rebuild resolves every difference by taking the fee app's word for it. A child moved yesterday and a child moved by mistake look identical to the code and completely different to the office |
| D-45 | A class change and a roll change on the same child are one difference, not two | A child who moved class almost certainly has a new roll number. Reporting both would double-count one event and make the list look worse than it is |
| D-46 | The parser reports problems; it does not reject a sheet | Task 2.2 has to show the school what is wrong before anybody publishes. A short row still parses as far as it goes, so one bad line shows as one bad line rather than an empty screen |
| D-47 | `SHORT_SUBJECTS` is not ported | `docs/SOURCES.md` says it is applied at render time and that applying it while parsing produces labels matching no subject row. A test asserts the long spellings survive the parse |
| D-48 | The import dry run keeps going after the first unresolved name | A run that stopped at the first unknown teacher would make the school fix one, re-upload, and find the next — nineteen times |
| D-49 | Publishing archives the previous version before publishing the new one | The partial unique index allows exactly one published version per session, so the other order is refused. Closing first means a failure leaves the session briefly unpublished — visible — rather than permanently blocked |
| D-50 | `teaching_assignments` is regenerated last in a publish, and replaced wholesale | Last, so an interruption leaves stale assignments for a live timetable rather than assignments for one that is not live. Wholesale, because an assignment the new timetable does not contain must disappear, and a diff would put the rule in a second place |
| D-51 | "Who is free" takes the whole teaching roster, not the teachers on the timetable | Somebody with no periods at all is the *most* free. Deriving the roster from the timetable is exactly the bug `timetable2025` worked around with a hard-coded reserve list |
| D-52 | Every screen under `(app)` must be a nav tab or listed in `NON_NAV_SCREENS` | The `ready` flag only guards items already in the nav list, so a screen left out entirely was invisible to it — which is how `/timetable` was briefly unreachable. A test now requires one or the other |
| D-53 | Which session it is, and what a class is called, are gated on *being staff* — not on `academics:view` | A teacher has no `academics:view`; it guards the screens that *edit* master data. Gating the session label on it meant a teacher opening their own register was refused before a single mark could be read. A class is called "Class 6 A" and knowing that is not a privilege |
| D-54 | `on_behalf_of` names the teacher the office stood in for, never the person pressing the button | `taken_by` already records who pressed it. Setting both to the same person makes the two columns agree and records nothing — which is the one fact the column exists to keep |
| D-55 | The monthly register prints; there is no PDF renderer | The browser already owns the paper size, the margins, the preview and the Devanagari fonts, and on every device the school uses "print" is also "save as PDF". A server-side renderer would mean shipping all four again to produce a worse copy of the same table |
| D-56 | Fee figures appear on the student card only for `collection:view` | A teacher does not need a family's dues in order to teach, and putting them on a screen opened in a corridor invites the conversation the office should be having. Fees and late fees stay separate figures either way (D-33) |
| D-57 | The offline queue is an external store read through `useSyncExternalStore`, not component state | `localStorage` changes without React knowing and has to be read on mount. As component state that means `setState` inside an effect — a cascading render, and the lint rule that caught it was right. It also makes a second tab's writes visible for free |
| D-58 | A `Localized` pair whose halves are identical renders one line, not two | Proper nouns are most of them: a class is "Class 6 A" and a teacher is their name in either language. "Class 6 A · Class 6 A" looks broken to exactly the readers who asked to see both |
| D-59 | The Today card distinguishes three kinds of nothing — school closed, no class of your own, everything already in | A card that counts rows renders all three as an empty box, and only one of them means "you are finished". Telling a teacher they are free when they are not is the failure this screen exists to prevent |
| D-60 | Marks are not on the student card yet, and there is no placeholder for them | PLAN 3.5 names them, but the assessment tables arrive in Phase 6.1. An empty section promising marks is a promise the data cannot keep |
| D-61 | The substitution engine is ported whole, minus its `localStorage` plan store and its two hard-coded name lists | The ranking, the tiers, the weights and the min-cost flow have run this school's cover for a year and came across unchanged. What did not: `createPlanStore` (plans are rows here, so pruning is a query and invalidation is `substitution_plans.version_id`), and `DEFAULT_SHIFTS` / `RESERVE_STAFF` / `DUTY_STAFF`, which named real teachers in source — rule 6 forbids that here, and a school that cannot change its own part-time hours without a deploy is worse off anyway. They are rows in `cover_policies` (task 4.4) |
| D-62 | Any read that can exceed a thousand rows is paged through `fetchAllRows()` | PostgREST caps a response at `max-rows` — 1000 on Supabase — with **no error and no warning**. The published timetable is 1038 slots, so `listSlots()` had been returning 1000 of them since Phase 2: thirty-eight lessons missing from every timetable view and invisible to the planner. The worst shape a limit can take is the one where every caller still looks correct |
| D-63 | A role with more screens than the bar holds gets a "More" hub as its fifth tab | The cap is five, because a sixth on a 360px phone puts every tab under 60px. The old `slice(0, 5)` met the cap by dropping the sixth screen entirely, which is how `/substitutions` became unreachable for admin, office and the academic coordinator the day it was added — the same class of bug as `/timetable` shipping with no tab. A test now asserts that everything a role may open is reachable somewhere |
| D-64 | A vacancy is one per class and period; a shared block absorbs an absence, a parallel elective does not | ELGA is one activity with five teachers, so one away leaves four teaching and no cover is needed. "Biology / Maths (X / Y)" is two lessons side by side with one cohort each, so one away leaves a cohort with nobody even though somebody is standing in the same room. One vacancy per (class, period) even when both halves of a parallel cell are away: the room needs one cover teacher, and the unique index says the same |
| D-65 | Publishing a plan notifies the covering teachers in the app, on their own Today card — not by WhatsApp | A cover nobody was told about is a class nobody turns up to, so publishing has to reach the teacher. An outbound send is a different thing with a different cost and a different failure mode; the AiSensy integration arrives in Phase 5.4 and the scheduled nudges in 7.2, and until then the app is where the school already looks each morning |
| D-66 | Anything a coordinator sets by hand is pinned by construction, and a pin survives regenerating | They decided it. The reason to work the cover out again is that something changed *around* their decision, not that they want it undone — so the pinned rows are read first, everything else is cleared, and the engine is handed the pins as work already done so it never double-books against one |
| D-67 | The seed scripts load the app's own parser and engine from source, through a 15-line `@/` resolver hook | Node 24 runs TypeScript by stripping types, which is enough for modules that are logic and types and nothing else. A reimplementation in `scripts/` would be a second parser and a second engine to keep in step, and seeded data the app could never have produced is worse than no seeded data. `plan.ts` and `labels.ts` are split along that line for this reason |
| D-68 | The token links are public by design, and the token is the whole authorization | The school has forty staff and four accounts. Asking the other thirty-six to remember a password is asking them not to answer, which is the problem Sampark was built to solve. Open is not unguarded: ~96 bits of entropy, one request per token and no navigation past it, every rejection the same 404, and a guess rate limited in Postgres with its own budget so a teacher never spends a guesser's and a guesser never hides inside hers |
| D-69 | An accepted answer lands in `vppsone.student_records`, never in the fee app | Sampark wrote master fields straight back to its own `students` table; rule 2 forbids that here. So `collection_fields.fee_column` is a **comparison**, not a destination: the form shows a teacher what the fee app holds, the review queue says when the two disagree, and a person changes the fee app deliberately, in the fee app |
| D-70 | An approved human answer outranks every import, forever, and the rule lives in code | A row can be edited, and no administrative convenience is worth the day a teacher discovers her approved correction was quietly undone by somebody re-importing last term's export. That is the difference between the tool being trusted and being abandoned |
| D-71 | A repeated phone number is never an error | Siblings share a parent's number — 134 numbers in the school Sampark was built for, 133 of them spanning more than one class. A uniqueness check would refuse the correct answer and teach a teacher the screen is wrong about her own class. The office sees a neutral "also on N other children" count; the teacher sees nothing |
| D-72 | Blank always means "no change", never "erase" | A teacher who skips a child has not answered for them. A form that read a skipped box as a deletion would empty a register on its first pass, and the register is what everything else is built on |
| D-73 | The review queue splits changes from confirmations, and confirmations are accepted in bulk | "This is already right" is a real and useful answer — somebody looked — but it is not a change, and a queue that made the office approve four hundred of them one at a time is a queue nobody finishes |
| D-74 | The public link renders in the language of the teacher it was made for | She has no session to read a preference from, so the language travels with the request. Rule 9 does not stop applying because somebody is not signed in |
| D-75 | A student document lives in a private bucket and is served through a signed URL that lasts a minute | A birth certificate with a guessable URL is a birth certificate anybody has. The path is keyed by child and by a random id rather than the filename, so two parents both sending `aadhaar.pdf` cannot collide and nothing is guessable. The permission is checked inside the function that mints the URL, because that is what hands out the capability |
| D-76 | A parent-facing message is never dispatched by the app | D-10, now executable: `mayDispatch()` refuses it whatever the screen believes, and the office opens it in their own WhatsApp instead. The day an automatic send goes out wrong it goes out wrong to four hundred families at once, with no recall. Staff messages may go through the provider, and even those need an `AISENSY_API_KEY` this repo does not carry — sending costs money, which rule 8 makes somebody else's decision |
| D-77 | "9 of 45" is a function, not two numbers around a translated "of" | English counts up from the part, Hindi counts down from the whole: `45 में से 9`. Interpolating in one fixed order renders backwards in one of the two languages, which is how the attendance register shipped wrong in Hindi in Phase 3 — caught in Phase 5 on a page that happened to render for a Hindi reader |
| D-78 | An exam has a paper per class per subject, not one max mark | PLAN §5 says "exams, subjects, max marks" as though a max belonged to the exam. It does not: a Class 5 English paper and a Class 10 English paper are different papers out of different totals, and one `max_marks` on the assessment would make the whole school sit the same one. `assessment_subjects` is the extra table that makes the rest of the phase possible |
| D-79 | A mark has three states, and every screen shows all three | Blank is nobody has entered it yet, Absent is a child who did not sit the paper, and a number is a number — including nought, which is a real mark somebody gave. A grid that collapsed blank and nought would make "who has not entered yet" unanswerable, which is the question the whole board exists to answer. `isEntered()` is the one place the rule lives, and the check constraint `marks_absent_has_no_value` stops the database holding two answers at once |
| D-80 | Every change to a mark is logged with both sides of it, and an unchanged save writes nothing | A mark that changes after a parent has seen it is the most sensitive write in this app, so "it was 68 last week" gets an answer that does not depend on anybody remembering. The log is append-only. A save that changes nothing is not a change, and a log padded with them is a log nobody reads |
| D-81 | A paper nobody has marked is out of both halves; an absence is a nought it is out of | Counting an unmarked paper as zero hands a family a percentage the child did not earn, and the mistake looks exactly like a bad result. An absence is different: the child sat the term and did not sit the paper, which is a real nought on a real report. The two cases are one line apart in `totalsFor()` and are the arithmetic everything else inherits |
| D-82 | A report card is a snapshot, not a view | A card is what the school handed a family on a day. Marks move afterwards — a correction, a re-mark, a paper found under a desk — and recomputing the card from today's marks would silently rewrite what a parent is holding. So an issued card stores its own copy of every number and grade it printed, and a reprint is a new card somebody asked for rather than yesterday's card quietly moving. `previewReportCard()` and `issueReportCard()` are separate functions for exactly this reason |
| D-83 | Weight applies to the percentage, never to a printed mark | A paper is out of what it is out of, and a family reading "34 out of 40" must see the number the teacher wrote — so `scored` and `outOf` stay the plain sums and add up by hand. What a school means by "the half-yearly counts double" is that it counts double *towards the term*, which is the percentage. `totals.weighted` says whether it changed anything, so a card only explains itself when the weights actually differ. This was written down before it was true: the column and the form field shipped applying to nothing, and the smoke test on seeded data is what caught it |
| D-84 | The grade bands are a default, and the screen says so | Nobody has told us what this school's bands are. Inventing them silently would put a letter on a document a family keeps, on somebody else's authority — so the bands are in one exported constant, the report screen carries a line saying they still need confirming, and changing them is one edit rather than a search |
| D-85 | There is no report card PDF — the browser prints it | PLAN 6.4 says "PDF". A print stylesheet is what a school with one printer and a phone in every pocket actually uses: nothing to install, nothing to generate server-side, and the page a coordinator checked on screen is the page that comes out. A PDF endpoint would be a second renderer to keep in step with the first, for a file nobody asked to store |
| D-86 | The app accepts a link Supabase minted, not only a password | `/auth/confirm` is the standard `token_hash` door, and it is the one the school actually needs: forty staff, four accounts, and the reason for that gap is not secrecy but that asking thirty-six people to remember a password is asking them not to use the thing (D-68). It cannot be used to *request* anything — there is no "send me a link" form anywhere, and only the service role can mint a token — so the endpoint is inert until somebody deliberately issues one. Both doors run the same `admitSignedInUser()`, so the profile and active checks cannot drift apart |
| D-87 | The signed-in screens are verified by signing in, with passwordless test accounts | Every phase from 3 to 6 shipped with nobody having looked at the authenticated screens; they were covered by route guards, SQL probes and unit tests, which is not the same thing. `npm run dev:login` mints a one-time link for a `@qa.vpps.local` account, refuses any other address, and refuses to run outside dev. The first pass through it found two bugs that no probe would ever have caught (D-88, D-89) |
| D-88 | A note that a screen works offline is not the same sentence as a report that it is offline | The Teach card rendered `attendanceOffline` — "there is no internet" — unconditionally, so every teacher on a working connection was told she had none. Present-tense status and standing capability are two messages, and the register form's conditional use of the first one was right all along |
| D-89 | A missing mark row and a blank mark row are the same state | `saveMark()` required an existing row before it would call a save unchanged, so a teacher who filled in one child and saved wrote a change-log entry for every child she had not reached — each reading "not entered → not entered" — and left an empty row behind for each. The comparison moved into `markChanged()` in the domain, where it is one function with tests, rather than a condition in a repo nothing could reach |
| D-90 | One run of a job at a time is a partial unique index, not a flag in the application | A lock held in memory does not exist when the application is two instances, which on Vercel it always is — two overlapping register-opening runs would each find no register and each create one. `job_runs_one_at_a_time` makes the database refuse the second insert, and the repo reads that refusal as "already running" rather than as an error, because two crons overlapping on a redeploy is an ordinary thing that happens |
| D-91 | A job that already succeeded today is skipped, and only a person can override it | This is what makes a cron that fires twice harmless — a retry, a redeploy, two regions. A failed run does not count as done, so the retry still runs, and a *skip* does not count either: treating it as the success would make a job that failed once look like a job that has worked every day since. The console's button forces it on the second press, after the first has said why, because opening a second set of registers should take two decisions |
| D-92 | Each job has its own token, derived from one secret, and an unconfigured deploy runs nothing | `HMAC(JOBS_SECRET, job)` gives four different tokens and none of them is the secret, so one can be handed to whatever triggers one job without handing over the other three, and a token that leaks out of a cron dashboard reveals nothing about the rest. Vercel's own `CRON_SECRET` bearer is accepted alongside it. With neither variable set the route refuses everything rather than accepting everything — "the jobs never ran" is a discoverable problem in a way that "anybody could run the jobs" is not. Every refusal is the same 404, so a guesser cannot learn which job names exist |
| D-93 | A job that swallows a query error reports a confident zero, so every read fails loudly | `attendance-reminder` reported "19 classes have no class teacher" on its first run. That was not a finding about the school: the query named `from_day`/`to_day` on a table whose columns are `effective_from`/`effective_to`, PostgREST returned an error, and `const { data } = await …` dropped it. A second wrong table name was hiding behind the same silence. Every read in `job-work.ts` now goes through `must()`, which throws — and `runJob()` turns that into a failed run somebody can see, rather than a cheerful number nobody questions |
| D-94 | A reminder is deduplicated per template, not per person | The first version skipped anybody who had received *any* message that day, so a teacher nudged about outstanding marks silently stopped being asked about her register. Two jobs ask two different questions about two different pieces of work, and "already asked" has to mean already asked *this* |
| D-95 | An embed names its foreign key whenever a table points at another one twice | `message_sends` references `staff_profiles` from both `requested_by` and `staff_profile_id`, so `staff_profiles(display_name)` has no answer: PostgREST refuses with "more than one relationship was found" rather than picking one. `/collection/messages` shipped in Phase 5 with that select and threw on every load until somebody signed in and opened it. Nothing else could have caught it — it typechecks, it is not a data problem, no unit test reaches PostgREST, and a SQL probe issues different SQL than the app does. `tests/embeds.test.ts` now reads the foreign keys out of the migrations and the selects out of the repos and compares them, so the check grows with the schema instead of being remembered |
| D-96 | A check that finds nothing has not passed, it has broken | Said twice in one day and worth writing down. The cutover check printed a tick after its regex was mangled and it found zero tables; `tests/embeds.test.ts` passed against the exact bug it was written for, because the repo is checked out CRLF on Windows and every pattern anchored on `\n` silently matched nothing. Both now assert that they found something before they report on it, and a new check gets tried against the broken state before it is trusted |
| D-97 | A guard that fires mid-stream cannot set a status code, and the smoke test has to know that | Once the shell has started streaming, `redirect()` in a Server Component emits `<meta id="__next-page-redirect" http-equiv="refresh">` instead of a 307. Read the status alone and every refusal in the app looks like a page that rendered — which is what the first run of `smoke:roles` concluded, about all six roles at once, and it would have been reported as a catastrophic permission failure. The classifier reads the meta tag. The side effect worth knowing: a refused screen shows its shell for about a second before bouncing, because the page began rendering before the guard resolved |
| D-98 | Every screen is opened as every role, and the expected answer is read out of the screen's own guard | `permissions.ts` says who may open what, and a unit test that checked a page against that map would be asserting the map against itself. `npm run smoke:roles` takes the permission out of each page's own `requirePermission()` call, works out what the map says should happen, signs in as all six roles and finds out what does — thirty-six screens by six roles, which is more combinations than anybody checks by hand and where the interesting ones are always the boring ones nobody thought to try |
| D-99 | A page guard does not guard the action underneath it | A Server Action is a separate POST to a generated endpoint, reachable by anybody holding the action id whether or not they can open the page it came from. The page and the action are two doors into the same room. `tests/action-guards.test.ts` reads the body of every exported async function in a `"use server"` file and requires a check inside it, with two files exempt on purpose: signing in, and the public collection link where the token is the authorization (D-68) |
| D-100 | A skipped run is written down | Returning quietly left the console unable to tell "the cron fired at 7:15 and found the work already done" from "the cron never fired at all" — and which of those it is happens to be the only question the console exists to answer. The skip row carries no summary, because the status badge already says "Already done" in the reader's own language; the card shows the numbers from the last run that actually did something, which is a different row whenever the last attempt was a skip |
| D-101 | Nothing is outstanding on a day the school was shut | The daily summary subtracted submitted registers from the number of classes, so every Sunday reported nineteen registers outstanding — nineteen teachers apparently having failed to take one, on a day nobody was there. Found by running the job for a Sunday rather than by reading it |

## Revision 2 decisions — 2026-09-17

| # | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| D-102 | PLAN revision 2 and R0–R8 are the active queue; preserve original history and archive original plan/kickoff | Original features are implemented but workflow parity and role UX are incomplete. Deferred live-cutover 7.3 must not stop test-data improvement work |
| D-103 | Working scope preserves original SamPark/School Fees apps, exposes explicit handoffs and completes native SamPark parity; fee operations remain in School Fees | Preserves existing architecture and the request that apps remain where they were. This is an explicit planning assumption, not a confirmed reply. PLAN §9 defines the alternative if native fees are selected |
| D-104 | Reopen earlier photo/workbook omissions as R4 parity tasks; port complete behavior rather than only shapes | Original SOURCES documents intentional omissions that explain missing workflows; green original phase rows do not prove source feature equivalence |
| D-105 | Existing guard-derived GET smoke is supplemented with independent role expectations and mutation/recovery workflows | A wrong page guard may agree with its own expected result; missing fixtures may otherwise skip without failing |
| D-106 | Keep per-account language and support single-language authored request content with optional translations | The inspected request form requires both title languages and sends reasons through English-only input, increasing work for nontechnical staff |
| D-107 | Performance changes require baseline and before/after measurement; deployment, browser verification and staff acceptance remain distinct | Proposed budgets and source inspection must not be reported as observed success; physical/provider tests need their own evidence |
| D-108 | Sibling app hostnames live in `src/platform/config/app-origins.ts`, are resolved from Vercel project metadata, and are re-checkable with `npm run verify:origins` | Two of the three hostnames a reader would guess are wrong, and one of them is dangerous: `schoolfees.vercel.app` answers 200 with `<title>Ecolage Desktop</title>` and belongs to a different company, while the school's fee app is served at `vpps-one.vercel.app`. A link written from memory would hand a member of staff a stranger's login form. The wrong hostnames are listed in code as `FOREIGN_ORIGINS` so a test can refuse them, and the right ones carry a string to look for in the response — because "the host answered" is not "the host is the application we meant" |
| D-109 | An origin's `environment` describes the **dataset behind it**, not its Vercel target, and an id may not cross datasets | VPPS One's Vercel *production* deployment is a `dev` origin, because rule 3 keeps it on schoolfees-dev. Both siblings are on live data. So every VPPS One → sibling deep link today crosses a boundary where the student id means nothing, and rule 3 forbids connecting to the live fee database to check. `idsTravelBetween()` returns false for every such pair and R1.1 builds the search-screen fallback around that, rather than shipping id-bearing URLs and discovering the 404s in testing |
| D-110 | The client-JS ratchet is measured from `.next/static`, not from the build output | Next.js 16.3.5 with Turbopack prints the route table without a First Load JS column, so the number PLAN §5 R7 asks to ratchet is not in the build log. `find .next/static -name '*.js' -printf '%s\n'` after a production build is reproducible and is what the R0.1 baseline of 1,293.2 KB across 37 files was taken with |
| D-111 | A missing sibling test environment is recorded as a gap, not engineered around | School Fees preview deployments do point at schoolfees-dev, which looked at first like the fee sandbox R6.2 needs — but every preview URL answers `302 → vercel.com/sso-api`, so it is not a destination a member of the office could open. SamPark has no test deployment at all. Inventing a substitute (a local fee app, a mocked destination) would produce evidence about something staff will never use, so R6.1 links the live fee app with an explicit label and R6.2 stays blocked on somebody creating a real one |
| D-112 | The QA teacher is bound to a teacher the published timetable already knows, chosen by rule rather than named in the repository | A teacher's periods come from `timetable_slots`, so an account the sheet has never heard of has no day no matter what else is written for it — which is why `qa.teacher@` could hold nothing for seven phases and 216 smoke combinations without anybody noticing (B-17). Giving it a day by editing the published timetable would corrupt the sheet; giving it one by binding the login to a profile the sheet already contains costs one column. The pair is picked by "most periods, ties by short name, second must share no class with the first" so that no member of staff is named here (rule 6) and the choice survives a new timetable. It refuses rather than pairing two teachers who overlap, because a fixture whose two teachers share Class 8 makes every cross-teacher leak test pass for the wrong reason |
| D-113 | Every QA fixture write records its previous value, and `qa:reset` replays the log backwards | The seeder has to touch rows it did not create, so "delete what I made" is not enough and "set it back to null" is a guess that quietly eats whatever was there. The undo log is also the only way PLAN §5 R0's "fixtures removable by run ID" and QA.md's "verify cleanup" can be answered with evidence instead of a claim: the proof that it works is 11 steps out, 11 steps back, the database compared row by row, and 11 steps out again picking the same two teachers. An unchanged save writes nothing and logs nothing (D-80), so a second seed records zero steps rather than filling the log with restorations of a value to itself |
| D-114 | The fixture cannot invent children, and says so rather than finding a way | The roster is `public.students` and rule 2 makes it SELECT-only. Emptying a class is possible because leaving is a VPPS One fact (`enrolments.status`), so `--edge-classes` can build a class of none and a class of one; a 40-child class and the 1,205-row list QA.md asks for cannot be built from here at all. Seeding the fee dev database is somebody else's decision, and the row-cap risk that made this matter (D-62) is already covered by the 1,038-row timetable read and `tests/paginate.test.ts` |
| D-115 | Which provider sends is chosen by `MESSAGE_PROVIDER`, the mock is refused on a live deployment, and every mocked id carries a `mock:` prefix | QA needs a 429, a timeout and a duplicate on demand, and a test student does not make a recipient phone a test phone (PLAN §1) — so the outcome comes from the destination's last digit and no packet leaves. Two guards make that safe rather than dangerous: a live deployment refuses the mock outright, because writing `sent` rows for messages nobody received would be discovered by a parent rather than by us; and the `mock:` prefix lands in `message_sends.provider_message_id`, so a row that was never really sent can never be read as one by a screen, a later query, or somebody reading the table at cutover. `duplicate` returns the **same** id every time, which is what makes a replayed delivery callback testable at all |
| D-116 | `periodNumber()` is the only place the period off-by-one lives | The sheet's first column is index 0 and `timetable_slots.period` stores it, so every screen has to add one. Six screens each remembered; the teacher's Today card — the screen she opens every morning — did not, so her first lesson read "0" and her last read "7". It shipped in Phase 3.1 and nothing could catch it, because the only teacher account in the test data had no periods to render. The fix is not the `+ 1`, it is removing the thing to remember: one function, used everywhere, and a test that scans every component for a hand-written `period + 1` and for a raw `{…period}` rendered as JSX children |
| D-117 | The role smoke compares three readings, and a disagreement between the code and the plan has to be owned by name | The map and the guards both come from the same repository, so a page that names the wrong permission agrees with itself and 216 green combinations say nothing (B-09). `tests/fixtures/role-expectations.ts` is PLAN §3 written out by hand, and the two kinds of disagreement are not the same thing: a screen disagreeing with the map is a bug, and the map disagreeing with the plan is a product question. The second one is only tolerable when somebody has looked at it, so an *unowned* drift fails the run and an owned one is printed every time with its owner. `--strict` is what the owning task turns on when it resolves one |
| D-118 | The browser journeys assert the rows, never the message | "Saved" is a `useState`. Every bug this app has actually had in a save path rendered perfectly while being wrong — the offline notice that lied (D-88), the empty marks rows for unreached children (D-89), the confident zero from a swallowed query error (D-93). So each journey reads the database back, and the refusal cases assert the **absence** of a row, which is the half a message cannot tell you about |
| D-119 | The workflow suite runs against a production build and is not part of `npm run check` | The states it tests — a save racing a reload, a streamed guard, a stale tab — behave differently under `next dev`'s compilation and error overlay, so `next dev` would be testing a different application. It stays out of `check` because it needs a dev database credential and a browser: `check` is what must pass before a merge, and this is what must pass before a phase gate (PLAN §5). Putting a service-role key in CI to merge the two is a decision, not a convenience |
| D-120 | A workflow test restores what it touched, and finding out what "untouched" means is part of writing it | The first teardown deleted a register it had created and left alone one it had found — and a register for today is always already there, because `open-registers` opens one per class every morning. So the run left Class 1 submitted and the next run failed with "there is nothing to do today", which reads like a product bug. Snapshotting the row and its marks and putting both back is the only version that survives being run twice, and the suite was run four times to prove it |
| D-121 | Every sibling URL is built by the app registry, and no other file in `src/` may contain a `vercel.app` hostname | The registry is only worth having if it is the *only* way, so the rule is a test that scans every source file rather than a convention. Two of the three hostnames a reader would guess are wrong and one of them answers 200 as a different company product (B-15, D-108) - a hostname typed from memory into one screen would be indistinguishable from a working link until a member of the office was standing in front of somebody else login form |
| D-122 | A link to another app carries an id only when both sides are looking at the same database, and says which kind of link it is | VPPS One is on schoolfees-dev and both siblings are on live data, so a student id from here means nothing there and rule 3 forbids connecting to the live fee database to check. The builder falls back to the destination own search screen, which is PLAN section 2 rule made into a function instead of a habit. It also returns `kind`, because "Find this child" and "Open this child" are different promises and shipping the second while doing the first is how a button stops being trusted. SamPark never gets a detail link in either direction: its student ids are its own, mapping them is R3.1 (S-10), and guessing by name is what PARITY.md forbids outright |
| D-123 | `apps:view` is a permission for being *offered* the way to another app, never for what that app allows | A launcher cannot grant a fee role and this one does not pretend to: the destination decides, and the card says so. PLAN section 3 puts Apps on the admin and office navigation and nowhere else - a teacher gets no fee cards and neither coordinator has an errand in SamPark. It is a permission rather than a hard-coded role list so navigation and access keep coming from the same map (D-26) |
| D-124 | Every app card carries the destination own environment badge, never this app | The header badge says "test" because VPPS One is on schoolfees-dev. Both siblings are pointed at the school live records, so a reader who took one badge to cover all three would be wrong about the two where it matters. Each card reads its own environment out of the registry and, when the two differ, says so in a sentence as well - and the hostname is printed in full under the button, unlinked, so the thing being opened can be read before it is opened |
| D-125 | There is one Home per role, resolved after the profile is known | PLAN section 3 gives a teacher `/teach` and everybody else `/dashboard`, and the difference matters because a teacher who signs in wants the register she came for, not a card with her own email address on it (B-03). The fallback cannot be a constant: the proxy stamps `?next=` from whatever was asked for, a bare visit leaves none, and only the profile says which Home is right - so `admitSignedInUser()` hands the role back with the admission rather than the caller running a second query that could disagree about who just signed in. A deep link still wins; the role Home is what happens when nothing was asked for |
| D-126 | Each role navigation bar has its own order, and a screen the plan names but the app lacks simply leaves the row shorter | One shared order gave every role Home, Teach, Cover, Timetable, Exams, so the office - who opens SamPark and the fee app several times a day - reached its work through More behind three screens it never uses, and R1.1 Apps tab never reached its bar at all (B-04). The rows follow PLAN section 3 navigation column. Where a screen it names does not exist yet (Students is R2.3, a teacher Requests inbox is R3.3) nothing is invented to fill the gap: a tab leading to a route that is not there is a 404 with the school name on it (D-26), and a shorter bar is honest about what has been built |
| D-127 | `view_only` does not hold `attendance:view` | It guards six screens - the teacher workspace, the register form, a class roster, the corrections queue, the monthly register and a child card - and every one is an editable form or a list of named children, which PLAN section 3 gives this role neither of. It also put a Teach tab on a viewer bar leading to an empty teacher workspace. Nothing in the repository could see it, because the map and the guards agreed with each other perfectly; it took the independent expectations in `role-expectations.ts` to notice (B-09, B-22). The attendance *figures* the plan does promise the role are a count on its own Home read under `dashboard:view` - a permission is about screens, not rows |
| D-128 | The desktop sidebar shows everything the role may open, grouped, with no More | The bar holds five because that is what a thumb can hit on a 360px phone. A 1440px window has room for all of them, and putting four behind a hub built for a thumb is the phone constraint following somebody to their desk (PLAN section 3). With nothing hidden, a More entry would lead to an empty page, so there is not one. Each group is a landmark named by its own heading, because three groups a screen reader cannot tell apart are one undifferentiated list again |
| D-129 | A title somebody typed is one required half and one optional one, and the fallback is visible | Every string in `src/messages/` is a pair because somebody sat down and wrote both halves once. A request title is not that: the office types it on a Tuesday afternoon for one round, and asking for it twice doubles the most common task in the app to buy a translation nobody asked for (B-05, PLAN section 3a). The half that is missing still has to render as something - all four screens did `language === "en" ? titleEn : titleHi`, which is a blank title - so `readAuthored()` falls back and returns whether it did. Saying so is the point: a Hindi reader shown an English title is not looking at a bug, and hiding that is how somebody decides the app is half-translated and stops trusting the rest of it |
| D-130 | `classLabelMap()` is built in school order, and nothing sorts a class label as text | `"Class 10".localeCompare("Class 2")` is negative, so four screens listed Class 1, Class 10, Class 11, Class 12, Class 2 and a teacher looking for Class 2 found it ninth (B-06). The right order already existed on `public.classes.sort_order` and `vppsone.class_aliases.sort_order` and was being thrown away by `[...map.entries()].sort(byLabel)`. A Map keeps its insertion order, so building it right means three screens stop sorting altogether and the fourth - a teacher own classes, a subset - ranks against it. A test scans every source file for the two shapes that caused it, because the fix is only worth having if the fifth screen cannot reintroduce it |
| D-131 | Changing your own language needs no permission, and each option is written in its own language | It writes one column on the row belonging to whoever is asking, found by their own `user_id`: there is no id in the form to tamper with, and changing your language cannot grant you anything. Until R1.3 the only way to change it was an admin opening your staff profile - so the setting that decides whether you can read the screen at all needed somebody else. The options read हिन्दी / English / हिन्दी + English rather than being translated into the reader current language, because somebody who has ended up on a screen they cannot read needs to recognise the word for their own. It lives in the top bar beside sign-out: both are about the person rather than the school, and a teacher bar is already full so More is not a tab she has |
| D-132 | The phone baseline measures the label, not the input, and it is a browser test rather than a review note | A checkbox is drawn 20px square inside a 48px row that is the target - the primitive says so - so measuring the input would fail every form in the app for a rule none of them break. Measuring the right box is what let the run find the one real failure on its first pass: the `PageHeader` back link at **20px**, the smallest target in the app, on the screen a teacher opens most, reached with a thumb while holding a register. No amount of reading the CSS would have produced that number |
| D-133 | A lesson is a period and a subject, and a combined one is a single row carrying its classes | The timetable stores a slot per class, so a subject taught to Classes 1–5 at once is five rows. Rendering them as five gave a teacher twenty entries for an eight-period day and told her she was in five rooms at 11:00 (B-20). She is in one. Two *different* subjects in one period stay two rows, because that is a clash and merging it would hide the thing worth seeing - and a combined lesson gets no open-class link at all, since offering one of the five would be picking a room at random |
| D-134 | The teacher home offers one next action, and offers none when there is nothing to do | PLAN section 1 asks for one prominent next action, and one is the point: a screen that offers three equally-weighted next steps has not decided anything, and the deciding is exactly the part a teacher standing in a corridor at 7:40 cannot do. The register comes first because it is the only thing with a deadline measured in minutes. When they are all in there is deliberately no fallback - "you are finished" is the most useful thing the screen can say, and inventing a task to fill the space is how it stops being believed |
| D-135 | A card whose number cannot be read honestly is not rendered, and a missing source shows a dash rather than a nought | The design package shows Open requests and Records to check on the teacher home. A teacher request inbox is R3.3 and does not exist, and records are a permission a teacher does not hold, so both cards are absent rather than zero - a zero is a claim. The same rule gives the lesson count a dash when no timetable is published: "there is no timetable" and "you have a free day" are different news and a 0 tells the wrong one. Lesson times are absent for the same reason: there is no bell schedule in this database (D-61), so periods are numbered rather than given plausible clock times |
| D-136 | Two same-typed parameters in a row become one named object | `classRoll(classId, sessionLabel)` typechecks whichever way round the two strings go, and passing them backwards failed at Postgres with `invalid input syntax for type uuid: "TEST-2026-27"` - a clear message, arriving as an error card on the screen a teacher opens every morning. The message was fine; the signature was the bug. One brace at seven call sites makes the mistake unrepresentable |
| D-137 | Every exception on a role home carries an owner, and a role with no honest count gets no queue at all | PLAN section 3 asks for "exceptions with counts and owners", and the owner is the half that makes the rest useful: a number with no name beside it is somebody else problem to everybody who reads it. The empty queues are the same rule from the other side - the exam coordinator wants incomplete papers and report readiness, neither of which has a cheap count yet, so that home shows nothing rather than something wrong; a teacher exceptions are already the top of `/teach`; and the viewer gets figures because PLAN section 3 gives that role read-only information and no per-child actions |
| D-138 | A count that could not be read is null and renders as a dash, and still counts as needing attention | D-93 recorded a job reporting a confident zero because `const { data } = await` swallowed a query error. On a dashboard that same mistake is worse: an administrator reading 0 concludes the school has nothing outstanding, which is the one wrong answer this screen can give. So each read is attempted on its own, a failure records its key, and the row says the count could not be read where the owner would go. `needsAttention()` treats null as needing attention, because nobody knows that it does not |
| D-139 | The directory is a GET form whose state is the URL, filtered and counted in Postgres | No client state means Back works, a bookmark works, a link pasted into WhatsApp opens the same list for whoever receives it, and the screen ships no JavaScript of its own - which is the difference between usable and not on the connection it is opened on. Filtering in the database rather than in the browser is the other half: the staff list is a few dozen and ships whole (D-29), but the roster will be a few thousand and PostgREST truncates a read at a thousand rows with no error at all (D-62), so a directory that fetched everything would work today and start silently hiding children the year the school grew. Scope comes from VPPS One own enrolments rather than `public.students.class_id`, which holds where a child is *today* and is overwritten on promotion |
| D-140 | A bare number is an exact admission number; anything with a letter in it is a label, and labels search the admission number too | Typing "12" must not return every child whose SR contains a 12, so bare digits match exactly. But **this school issues alphanumeric SR numbers** - `TEST-CL6-002` - which never reach that branch, and the first version searched labels against names only: pasting an SR from a spreadsheet found nothing and read as "that child is not here". Found by typing one into the running app rather than by reading the code. The loose branch searches `admission_no` alongside the names now, and a test reads the repo query back so the rule cannot be quietly dropped |
| D-141 | Every stored value says where it came from, and an unknown source reads as itself | An approved human correction and an imported figure look identical once they are both text on a card, and which one it is decides whether somebody rings the parent to check (S-10). `record_source` is plain text in the database so a later version can add one, and a value the app has never heard of renders as its own word rather than being forced into one of the three known labels: saying "an import" about something that was not one is worse than saying a word nobody recognises |
| D-142 | A request freezes who was asked and what they were shown, at the moment it is created | The roll and the prefilled values were re-read from `enrolments` and `student_records` on every open, so moving a child between classes changed the sheet a teacher was halfway through, and an office correction changed what "the number we hold" meant on a link that had already gone out (B-07, S-02). Neither reads as an error; both look exactly like working software. A request is a question asked at a moment, and `collection_request_members` is that moment - `snapshot` is the diff base and is never re-derived, so "old value to new value" on the review screen still refers to what the teacher actually saw. The freeze runs after the fields are attached, because the snapshot is per field, and it upserts rather than inserts so a fan-out that failed halfway is finished by running it again |
| D-143 | A request with no frozen members falls back to deriving its roll | Every request created before the migration has no member rows, and the alternative to deriving is an old link rendering as an empty class. Reading nothing is a worse answer than reading a rebuilt one: the office can still see what was asked, and the only thing lost is a guarantee that link never had. New rounds always freeze, so the fallback shrinks to a fixed set of rows rather than being a permanent second code path - and the workflow test uses it deliberately, as the control that reproduces the defect |
| D-144 | `audience_kind` is plain text with no enum and no check | An enum costs a migration for a fifth kind, and every reader - the resolver, the board, the token routes, the review queue - carries this value through unchanged. An unrecognised kind is a row written by a later version, not corruption, so `audienceKindLabel()` returns it as its own word rather than throwing at somebody trying to read a request. The one thing that *is* constrained is a class request without a class, because dropping `class_id` NOT NULL to let a house span classes would otherwise let one through |
| D-145 | One link per group per round is a unique index, not a check somebody remembers | `(round_id, audience_kind, audience_label)` unique is what makes Resume safe: a fan-out that failed halfway is finished by creating the scopes that are missing, and a double-tapped Resume racing itself hits the index instead of minting a second token for a group that already has one. It is also why `MASTER_AUDIENCE_LABEL` is a constant - a fixed label is what turns "one master link per round" into a database guarantee rather than a convention |
| D-146 | A SamPark deep link needs a mapping row **and** matching datasets, and two mappings count as none | SamPark keys its students on its own text id, so there is no arithmetic that turns a fee UUID into one (S-10). `vppsone.external_student_ids` supplies the missing half, and the environment rule from D-122 still applies on top of it: a mapping matched on schoolfees-dev pointed at live records would not 404, it would open a *different child*, quietly, with their name on it. Two rows where there should be one is treated as no mapping at all, because a link that depends on row order will one day pick the other. Nothing populates the table yet - the matcher is R4.1 - so the link still degrades to search, which is now a missing row rather than a missing mechanism |
| D-147 | A test that changes the world under a live link also has to reproduce the bug it closes | `tests/e2e/collection-freeze.spec.ts` moves a child out of the class and corrects a value while a link is open, then asserts the page did not notice. That would pass just as well against code that never froze anything, because nothing in it shows the freeze is what did the work. So it deletes the member rows and reloads - which is not a hypothetical, it is the legacy path `rollForRequest` still takes - and asserts the roll shrinks and the correction leaks through. The fix and the defect are thirty lines apart, and the day the fix regresses the control fails with it |
| D-148 | The embed check reads `alter table` as well as `create table` | It was written after `/collection/messages` shipped broken for two phases on an ambiguous embed, and it stayed green while R3.1 gave `collection_requests` a *third* foreign key to `staff_profiles` and broke every `/collection` screen for every role (B-26). A table gains most of its second and third references by `alter table`, so reading only the birth block made the check strongest on the day a table was written and blind ever after. `collection_requests` is now asserted to be in the ambiguous set, which is also what proves the new half of the parser runs at all |
| D-149 | A confirm field is what a freeze test asserts against, because a collect field renders nothing to assert | The first version of the workflow test used `mother_phone` and checked that a correction made after issue was absent from the page. It passed - and would have passed against any code at all, because `mother_phone` is a collect field and a collect field prefills nothing. The assertion was about the absence of something never rendered. `guardian_phone` is a confirm field, so what the school holds is visible text, and "she was shown the old number" becomes something a test can actually look at |
| D-150 | An audience is OR within a dimension and AND across them, and an audience with nothing set selects nobody | Ported from SamPark `students.ts` rather than invented, after the first version of this file gave an audience a single `kind` and could not express what the office means by "classes 6 to 8, Rana Pratap house" - the union reading of that sends to the whole school. The other half is the failure mode: a half-built audience has to select **nobody**, because a missing filter that widened the blast radius is a send nobody chose, and `allEnrolled` is the deliberate way to say everybody. `parseAudience()` is tolerant in one direction only for the same reason - a value it cannot read is dropped, and dropping narrows |
| D-151 | What a round selects and what one link is scoped to are two different things | An *audience* is the selection; a *scope* is what one of a round's links covers, because a round fans out to one link per group. The schema always had them apart - `collection_rounds.audience` against `collection_requests.audience_kind` and `audience_label` - and conflating them in the domain is what made `groupCount()` count ticked classes instead of resolved groups. An uploaded list of forty children spread over six classes is six links, and only resolving the roster can say so, so the count takes scopes rather than a selection |
| D-152 | Two gap filters mean the children missing **both**, and the sentence shown to a teacher says "and" | It is the same AND-across-dimensions rule the audience follows (D-150), and reading it as "any" would send to more children than the office picked. The wording is the part that is easy to get wrong and is not a wording problem: "no phone number **or** date of birth" describes a bigger set than the round covers, so a teacher is told she is being asked about different children than she is. More than two are counted rather than listed, following SamPark - a four-item list in a WhatsApp message is a sentence nobody reads to the end |
| D-153 | A count and its noun agree in number, through one function | Both languages inflect and Hindi is the one that gets forgotten: "1 children" is obviously wrong and somebody fixes it, while "1 कक्षाओं में" reads as slightly broken to every Hindi speaker in the school and is invisible to everybody reviewing the code in English. It shipped for exactly that reason (B-28). A function rather than two message keys, for the reason `countOfPair()` is one - the number moves, the noun changes with it, and a component choosing between two keys by hand is one that will eventually only remember to do it in English |
| D-154 | The round builder is two forms on one page, and the count is a server render of the real resolver | The office decides whether to send by reading a number off this screen, so it has to be the resolver answer rather than arithmetic the browser did on what it happened to have loaded - the day those are two code paths is the day a preview says nine and the round covers eleven. A `GET` form changes the URL and a `POST` form carries the same selection as hidden fields, so the round created is the round previewed. The cost is a page load per tick; the return is a screen that ships no JavaScript of its own and works on the connection it is actually opened on, with Back and a shareable URL (D-139) |
| D-155 | The ask list reads CSV, and the upload is two steps where the first creates nothing | `SOURCES.md` records that SamPark `excel.ts` was not ported: a spreadsheet library to parse a file Excel already exports is a dependency bought for nothing. The BOM and CRLF are the part kept, because without them Excel opens the Hindi notes column as mojibake and a template whose notes cannot hold Hindi is a template for one language. The two steps are the whole feature: a list of thirty-five that quietly becomes a round of thirty-one is the worst thing this can do, because the office believes it asked about everyone and the four it dropped are the four whose rows were already wrong. Every non-blank row comes back matched or named, by the row number Excel shows down the left |
| D-156 | A child who has left is matched and then refused, rather than never found | The matcher loads the whole school rather than the roll, so a row naming somebody who has left reads as "this child is not on this session roll" instead of "no such SR number" - which would send the office looking for a typo that is not there. The resolver only ever takes enrolled children either way, so the refusal is real; what changes is whether the office can tell the two failures apart |
| D-157 | A filter with no data behind it is not offered | `public.students.house` and `transport_route_id` exist and are empty for all 139 rows on schoolfees-dev, and rule 2 forbids filling them. The resolver handles both dimensions, because it will need to; the builder shows no chips for them, because a chip that could only ever return nobody teaches the office that the filters do not work. Recorded as B-31 rather than worked around, and the ownership half is B-32 - `class_teachers` covers classes and nothing covers a house or a route, so such a round would have no recipient to fan out to |
| D-158 | A frozen list is the whole audience, and only `enrolled` still applies | ANDing an explicit `studentIds` list back together with the filters it came from is the nondeterminism freezing exists to remove: six numbers arrive overnight, a Resume re-resolves, and it finishes a round covering a smaller set than the links it is completing - while a class whose last gap was filled gets no link at all and the board still says nineteen groups. Ported verbatim in intent from SamPark `audienceWhere`. The one filter that survives is the roll: a child who has left is not somebody to ask a teacher about, whatever a list typed last term says (D-156). This was a real defect (B-33) that no test would have caught, because the failure is a *smaller* number and every screen would have rendered it without complaint |
| D-159 | House and route ownership is its own table; classes stay in `class_teachers` | Merging them would mean either dropping that table's `role`, `effective_from`/`effective_to` and `replaced_reason` - which exist because a class teacher is replaced mid-year and the register has to know when - or bolting four columns that mean nothing for a bus route onto every row. One source of truth per kind, and `ownersFor()` reads both. Matching is exact rather than by prefix, because several bus routes differ only by a parenthesised suffix and a loose match hands one route's children to another route's in-charge (B-32) |
| D-160 | A group has one owner, more than one, or nobody, and only the first is acted on | A link sent to the wrong teacher is a group that never gets collected and nobody notices until the due date, so none of the three answers is a guess. Two owners is a *choice for this round* rather than a mistake to clean up: both people really do hold it, and dropping one on the strength of a send would be a deletion nobody asked for. A teacher with no number blocks the group as well, and it is caught while planning rather than at insert time - a link nobody can be sent is a roster frozen for nothing, and catching it early is what lets the screen offer a box to type one into |
| D-161 | Who gets left out is returned beside the groups, never inferred from two totals | House is recorded for about a third of a school and bus route for about half, so grouping a house-wise send by house silently drops every child with no house on record - and those are exactly the children whose records are thinnest. The office believes it asked about everyone and nobody finds out. So `unassigned` is its own list with names in it, `blocked` is another, and the screen states both before anything is created. A discrepancy between two numbers is not a report; it is something somebody has to notice |
| D-162 | The fan-out is not one transaction, and resume is the only path | Per-link atomicity is kept - a link whose roster fails to freeze deletes itself, so none ever opens to an empty list. But if link seven of eleven fails the first six are kept: six live tokens with frozen rosters are six classes the office can send to right now, and deleting them to satisfy an all-or-nothing aesthetic destroys real work and buys nothing, because the office would simply do it again. There is no separate resume, because a resume *is* create-the-groups-that-are-missing - making it the only path means the code that runs after a failure is the code that ran before it, rather than a second implementation nobody exercises until something has already gone wrong. Sequential rather than concurrent, and the unique index on (round_id, audience_kind, audience_label) is what makes a double tap safe |
| D-163 | The board records what the office did, and this app sends nothing | VPPS One does not message a parent or a teacher (D-10, D-76). "Handed over" and "chased" are the office writing down what it did in WhatsApp itself, so the board reflects what actually happened rather than what this app attempted. Handing over happens once, so `sent_at` is both when and whether; a chase happens again every week the answers do not arrive, so the useful facts there are the most recent one and how many there have been - deliberately a different shape |
| D-164 | Staleness is measured against the frozen value, and only when something is held now | The submission carries what the teacher was shown (D-142); comparing against a value re-read at review time would make every answer look current, which is the bug freezing exists to remove. The second half is what stops the flag being useless: the warning exists to stop the office silently reverting somebody correction, so with no current value there is nothing to revert and the answer simply fills a hole. Flagging that case put a warning on four rows out of four in the dev fixture (B-37), and a flag on every row is a flag nobody reads |
| D-165 | A superseded answer is marked, never deleted, and a decided one is never superseded | Two rounds asking the same class for the same thing is ordinary - the office asks in April, half come back, and it asks the stragglers again in June - so one child and one field can carry two pending answers. The later one wins, but deleting the earlier leaves a tidy queue and no history, and "what did she say in April" is a real question when June answer looks wrong. An already accepted or rejected row is never touched, because rewriting a decision after the fact makes the audit trail a record of what the app thinks rather than of what anybody did. Two answers with the same timestamp supersede nothing: picking one by row order is how a queue starts depending on which way a query sorted |
| D-166 | "What the school holds" is one function, used by the gap filter, the freeze and the review queue | Three screens asking the same question three ways is three chances for them to disagree about what "missing" means, and the disagreement is invisible - each screen looks right on its own. `heldValue()` is VPPS One own accepted answer then the fee column, and B-37 is what a second, shorter implementation of it cost: a review queue that called four answers out of four stale because it only knew about half the sources |
| D-167 | The review queue names the group an answer came from, not the class on the request | A house or route link has no class at all, so a queue printing `class_label` prints a dash on every one of them - and the office reading it cannot tell which group is behind. `audience_label` is what the link itself said, which is the only thing the teacher and the office both saw |
| D-168 | "Accounts staff start in School Fees" is an explicit per-account preference, never an inferred role | The design package asks for it twice and warns against the obvious implementation twice: "do not silently turn `office` into `accountant`", "never derive account type from an email, a menu label, or a mock query parameter". `accountant` is the fee app identity model and nothing in this repository can grant it (rule 2, PLAN section 3, D-6); half the office are not in accounts, so an office account landing on a fee desk because somebody matched a mailbox is access invented by inference. So `staff_profiles.preferred_start` holds a choice the person makes about themselves, and `startOptionsFor()` offers only what `allowedFor(role)` and the app registry already allow - choosing School Fees opens a door that was already on the Apps hub, and the fee app still decides what happens on the other side of it |
| D-169 | A stored start screen is re-resolved on every read, and a stale one is ignored rather than honoured | The alternative is sending somebody to `/access-denied` every morning after a role change, or worse, still offering a fee desk to an account that was moved out of the office last term. Null - "your role own Home" - is the absence of a choice rather than a choice of the default, so a teacher promoted to office inherits the office Home without anybody editing a column. The server action re-checks the posted value against the same function that built the form, so a value typed in by hand is refused rather than stored |
| D-170 | A valid deep link always beats the start screen | Somebody who followed a link to a particular register and had to sign in on the way is asking for that register, not for their landing page. It is the half most likely to be lost when a landing rule is added, and it matters most for exactly the account the feature is for: an accounts member of staff who clicks a VPPS One link must reach what they clicked rather than being bounced out to the fee app |
| D-171 | `isSafeInternalPath()` is a whitelist of shapes, and a backslash is refused anywhere | `?next=` is whatever was in the URL that bounced off the proxy. `//evil.example` was already refused; `/\evil.example` was not, and URL parsing for http(s) reads a backslash as a slash - so it is the same protocol-relative redirect with one character changed (B-37). It was not exploitable only because the one caller assigned it to `URL.pathname`, which percent-encodes - and fixing B-36 removed that accident. Nothing in this app has a backslash in a route, so refusing one everywhere costs nothing and removes a parsing rule to be right about. Control characters go with it, because a newline in a `Location` header is a header split |
| D-172 | The administrator home is one screen, and the fee line on it carries no amount | The question an administrator opens with is "is the day going normally, and what needs me", and answering it on two screens makes them ask it twice. `attention.ts` keeps the decisions and `overview.ts` adds the figures beside them - four fractions and a round, which is what "attendance, collections, SamPark, cover and exam readiness side by side" is once it has to be true rather than illustrative. The fee row is present and says it is unavailable: the handoff is explicit that a missing fee source shows a next step and **never** a rupee figure, and a zero on this screen is a number an administrator could act on and would be wrong every day until R6 connects a real source. A test asserts no rupee symbol can reach it |
| D-173 | The teacher home says "first lesson", not "next lesson" | `timetable_slots` holds a period index and there is no bell schedule in this database (D-61, D-135), so nothing here knows what time it is in the school day. The atlas shows "Next: English  09:10"; implementing that means either inventing a clock time or guessing which period is current from one that does not exist. Naming the day first lesson and its period number is the true version of the same card |
| D-175 | A heading over rows is a `Panel`; a heading over anything else stays a `Section` | Seven of the atlas screens are the same composition — a card whose head carries a title and a context line, and whose body is hairline-divided rows. Fifteen screens had each built their own list instead, which is a different density on every one and reads as a different product. DESIGN-SYSTEM section 7 asks for shared component contracts precisely "to prevent inconsistent buttons, corner radii and density". The split is by content, not by looks: rows go in a Panel, free-form content stays in a Section, and a row that does not fit on a row — a review diff with a decision form in it, a cover slot with its candidate list — stays a Card, because forcing it into a row would delete what makes it worth opening |
| D-176 | A `Panel` is a `<ul>` and a `PanelRow` is an `<li>`, with the link inside the item | Every panel in this app holds a list of like things: children, staff, classes, exams, requests. The first version used a `div` with `divide-y`, which looks identical and makes a register fifteen unrelated paragraphs to a screen reader (B-42). The link goes inside the `li` rather than being it, because a list whose items are themselves anchors is announced as a list of links rather than of things — and because that is what a test counting `getByRole("listitem")` expects, which is how the mistake was caught the same hour it was made |
| D-177 | The attendance status controls sit beside the name from `sm`, and under it below that | Four buttons and a name on one line at 360px leaves nothing readable, so the phone keeps them stacked. A desk does not have that problem, and the atlas's register puts them on the row — which halves the scroll for a class of forty and is the difference between one screen and three. One breakpoint, no second component |
| D-174 | The supporting column is a layout, not a second page, and only the administrator and the teacher have one | Wide windows get the main task plus a 320-360px column beside it (DESIGN-SYSTEM section 3). `HomeColumns` renders one column when there is nothing to put beside the work, so every other role home is unchanged rather than growing an empty rail - and on a phone the column becomes the sections that follow, in the order somebody reads them in a corridor. The shell max width went from 1024 to 1280 for it: at 1024 a 224px sidebar plus a 340px column leaves the main column narrower than a phone |
| D-178 | The fee app is ported into this repo, and fee data is writable | Janmejay's call, made after the conflict with rule 2 was put to him: "eventually the vpps one will become the main app for all and we will close fees app once the vpps one is complete... the accountant should see the exact fee app as now in vpps one". So this is a full native port — 709 files, 48 screens, 20 modules — not a design refresh and not a read-only mirror.
